Dyadic Partition-Based Training Schemes for TV/TGV Denoising
Authors: Elisa Davoli, Rita Ferreira, Irene Fonseca, José A. Iglesias
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
Due to their ability to handle discontinuous images while having a well-understood behavior, regularizations with total variation (TV) and total generalized variation (TGV) are some of the best-known methods in image denoising. However, like other variational models including a fidelity term, they crucially depend on the choice of their tuning parameters. A remedy is to choose these automatically through multilevel approaches, for example by optimizing performance on noisy/clean image pairs. In this work, we consider such methods with space-dependent parameters which are piecewise constant on dyadic grids, with the grid itself being part of the minimization. We prove existence of minimizers for fixed discontinuous parameters under mild assumptions on the data, which lead to existence of finite optimal partitions. We further establish that these assumptions are equivalent to the commonly used box constraints on the parameters. On the numerical side, we consider a simple subdivision scheme for optimal partitions built on top of any other bilevel optimization method for scalar parameters, and demonstrate its improved performance on some representative test images when compared with constant optimized parameters.

Introduction

A fundamental problem in image processing is the restoration of a given “noisy” image. Images are often deteriorated due to several factors occurring, for instance, in the process of transmission or acquisition, such as blur caused by motion or a deficient lens adjustment.

A well-established and successful approach for image restoration is hinged on variational PDE methods, where minimizers of certain energy functionals provide the sought “clean” and “sharp” images. In the particular case where the degradation consists of additive noise, these energy functionals usually take the form1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} E(u):= \Vert u - u_\eta \Vert ^p_X + R_\alpha (u)\quad \text {for } u\in {\widetilde{X}}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(u):=‖u-uη‖Xp+Rα(u)foru∈X~,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη represents the given noisy image and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{X}$$\end{document}X~ is the class of possible reconstructions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη. The first term in (1.1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert u - u_\eta \Vert ^p_X$$\end{document}‖u-uη‖Xp, is the fidelity or data fitting term that, in a minimization process, controls the distance between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u$$\end{document}u and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη in some space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X$$\end{document}X. The second term, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_\alpha (u)$$\end{document}Rα(u), is the so-called filter term, and is responsible for the regularization of the images. The parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α is often called a tuning or regularization parameter, and accounts for a balance between the fidelity and filter terms.

A milestone approach in imaging denoising is due to Rudin, Osher, and Fatemi [60], who proposed (in a discrete setting, later extended to a function space framework in [1, 18]) an energy functional of the type (1.1) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X:=L^2(Q)$$\end{document}X:=L2(Q), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p:=2$$\end{document}p:=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{X}}:= BV(Q)$$\end{document}X~:=BV(Q), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_\alpha (u):= \alpha TV(u,Q)$$\end{document}Rα(u):=αTV(u,Q) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\subset \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}Q⊂R2 is the image domain and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ TV(u,Q)$$\end{document}TV(u,Q) is the total variation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q of a function of bounded variation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV(Q)$$\end{document}u∈BV(Q). Precisely, given an observed noisy version \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta \in L^2(Q) $$\end{document}uη∈L2(Q) of a true image, the ROF or TV model consists in finding a reconstruction of the original clean image as the solution of the minimization problem1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \min _{u\in BV(Q)} \Big \{\Vert u - u_\eta \Vert ^2_{L^2(Q)} +\alpha TV(u,Q)\Big \}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minu∈BV(Q){‖u-uη‖L2(Q)2+αTV(u,Q)}.A striking feature of this model is that it removes noise while preserving images’ edges. This model has been extended in several ways, including higher-order and vectorial settings to address color images, and gave rise to numerous related filter terms seeking to overcome some of its drawbacks, such as blurring and the staircasing effect (see, for instance, [4, 10, 22] for an overview).

In a nutshell, the TV model yields functions u that best fit the data, measured in terms of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm, and whose gradient (total variation) is low so that noise is removed. The choice of the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α plays a decisive role in the success of this and similar variational approaches, as it balances the fitting and regularization features of such models. In fact, higher values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α in (1.2) lead to an oversmoothed reconstruction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη because the total variation has to be “small” to compensate for high values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α; conversely, lower values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α in (1.2) inhibit noise removal and, in particular, the reconstructed image provided by (1.2) converges to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_\eta $$\end{document}uη as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}α→0 (see [34]).

In principle, the “optimal” parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α needs to be chosen individually for each noisy image, which makes such models require additional information to be complete. To address this issue, a partial automatic selection of an “optimal” parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α was proposed in [34, 35] (see also [23, 24, 39, 61]) in the flavor of machine learning optimization schemes. This automatic selection is based on a bilevel optimization scheme searching for the optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α that minimizes the distance, in some space, between the reconstruction of a noisy image and the original clean image. In this setting, both the noisy image, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη, and the original clean image, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_c$$\end{document}uc, are known a priori and called the training data. The rationale is to use the same parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α to reconstruct noisy images that are qualitatively similar to that of the training scheme and corrupted by a similar type and amount of noise and are thus expected to require a similar balance between fitting and regularization effects.

In the context of the TV model in (1.2), one such bilevel optimization scheme reads as follows. Here, and in the sequel, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R+ stands for the set of positive real numbers, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞). Moreover, for minimization problems over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R+ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^+ \times \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}R+×R+, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{arginf}\,}}$$\end{document}arginf instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {argmin}}$$\end{document}argmin to include the case where the infimum would be attained at the boundary of these open sets.1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{TV}\; \mathbf{TV\; learning\; scheme}&\end{aligned}$$\end{document}(LS)TVTVlearningschemeLevel 1. Find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \bar{\alpha }=\textrm{arg inf}\left\{ \int _Q |u_c-u_\alpha |^2\;\text {d}x:\,\alpha \in \mathbb {R}^+\right\} ; \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α¯=arg inf∫Q|uc-uα|2dx:α∈R+;Level 2. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}α∈R+, find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} u_\alpha = {\text {argmin}}\left\{ \int _{Q}|u_\eta -u|^2\;\text {d}x+\alpha TV(u,Q):\,u\in BV(Q)\right\} . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uα=argmin∫Q|uη-u|2dx+αTV(u,Q):u∈BV(Q).This approach yields a unified way of identifying the best fitting parameters for every class of training data lying in the same \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-neighborhood. However, the learning scheme (1.3) does not address a major drawback of the TV and similar models using scalar regularization parameters. In fact, it does not take into account possible inhomogeneous noise (occurring, e.g., in parallel acquisition in magnetic resonance imaging [38]) and other local features in a given deteriorated image that would benefit from an adapted treatment.

A solution to this issue consists in resorting to adaptive methods and varying fitting parameters instead. The mathematical literature in this direction is vast, from which we single out the following contributions: [49, 58] for results in the finite-dimensional case and for optimal image filters, [33] for bilevel learning in function spaces and development of numerical optimization, [29, 30, 51–53] for a study of optimal regularizers, [54] for a bilevel analysis of novel classes of semi-norms, [55] for an approach via Young measures, and [14, 26, 37, 42] and the references therein for an overview.

A relevant question in image reconstruction (as pointed out in [50], among others) is the possibility of adapting the fitting parameters to the specific features of a given class of noisy images by performing, e.g., a stronger regularization in areas which have been highly deteriorated and by tuning down the filtering actions in portions that, instead, have been left unaffected.

Here, starting from the ideas in [50], we propose space-dependent learning schemes that locally search for the optimal level of refinement and the optimal regularization parameters. The optimal level of refinement translates into finding an optimal partition of the noisy image’s domain that takes into account its local features. Precisely, as before, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=(0,1)^2$$\end{document}Q=(0,1)2 represents the images’ domain. We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}$$\end{document}L is an admissible partition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q if it consists of dyadic squares, each of which we often denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L$$\end{document}L (see Sect. 2 for a more detailed description of these partitions). Note that an admissible partition might be more or less refined in different parts of the domain. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {P}$$\end{document}P the class of all such admissible partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}$$\end{document}L of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q. Remark 1.1

(i) We observe that by taking the infimum in (1.5), the corresponding parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _L$$\end{document}αL is always well defined. Remark 1.3

(box constraint as a stopping criterion) To prove the existence of a solution to the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4), we adopt the usual box-constraint approach in which we replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}α∈R+ by1.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \alpha \in \bigg [c_0,\frac{1}{c_0}\bigg ]\quad \text { for some } c_0\in (0,1). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α∈[c0,1c0]for somec0∈(0,1).In this case, the analog of (1.5) becomes1.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{\alpha }_L=\inf \left\{ {{\,\textrm{arginf}\,}}\left\{ \int _L |u_c- u_{\alpha ,L}|^2\;\text {d}x:\,\alpha \in \big [c_0,\tfrac{1}{c_0}\big ]\right\} \right\} .\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α¯L=infarginf∫L|uc-uα,L|2dx:α∈[c0,1c0].Under some assumptions on the training data, we prove in Subsect. 3.4 (see Theorem 1.4) that this box constraint is equivalent to the existence of a stopping criterion for the refinement of the admissible partitions as in Definition 1.2.

provided by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω with (1.5) replaced by (1.11).

(i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV(u_c,L) < TV(u_\eta ,L)$$\end{document}TV(uc,L)<TV(uη,L);

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV(u_c,L) < TV(u_\eta ,L)$$\end{document}TV(uc,L)<TV(uη,L);

(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert u_\eta - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(L)} <\Vert [u_\eta ]_L - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(L)} $$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(L)2<‖[uη]L-uc‖L2(L)2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[ u_\eta ]_L=\frac{1}{|L|}\int _L u_\eta \;\text {d}x. $$\end{document}[uη]L=1|L|∫Luηdx.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert u_\eta - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(L)} <\Vert [u_\eta ]_L - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(L)} $$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(L)2<‖[uη]L-uc‖L2(L)2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[ u_\eta ]_L=\frac{1}{|L|}\int _L u_\eta \;\text {d}x. $$\end{document}[uη]L=1|L|∫Luηdx.

Next, we state our main theorem regarding existence of solutions for the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4). We state this result under the box-constraint condition. However, in view of Theorem 1.4, this result holds true under any stopping criterion for the refinement of the admissible partitions, and in particular if the training data satisfies the conditions (i) and (ii) above.

Theorem 1.5

(Existence of solutions to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω) There exists an optimal solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^*$$\end{document}u∗ to the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4), whenever (1.5) is replaced by (1.11) for some fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0 \in (0,1).$$\end{document}c0∈(0,1).

The proofs of Theorems 1.4 and 1.5 are presented in Sect. 3, where we also explore alternative stopping criteria.

As shown in [47, Theorem 2.4.17], given a positive, bounded, and Lipschitz continuous function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega :Q\rightarrow (0,\infty )$$\end{document}ω:Q→(0,∞) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \omega \in BV(Q;\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}∇ω∈BV(Q;R2), the solution of (1.7) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{\mathscr {L}}$$\end{document}ωL replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω may exhibit jumps inherited from the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω that are not present in the data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη, see Fig. 2 for a numerical example. Because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{\mathscr {L}}$$\end{document}ωL in Level 2 is constructed using the local optimal parameters given by Level 3, we heuristically expect that, in most applications, these extra jumps do not induce clearly visible artifacts. However, this possible issue has led us to consider two alternative adaptive space-dependent learning schemes.

First, we consider a learning scheme based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{\mathscr {L}}$$\end{document}ωL replaced by a smooth regularization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\omega _\epsilon )_{\mathscr {L}}$$\end{document}(ωϵ)L (see the regularized weighted TV learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega _\epsilon }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVωϵ in (1.12)). Second, using the fact that the minimizer in (1.6) coincides with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\text {argmin}}\left\{ \frac{1}{\alpha }\int _{L}|u_\eta -u|^2\;\text {d}x+ TV(u,L)\!:\,u\in BV(L)\right\} , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}argmin1α∫L|uη-u|2dx+TV(u,L):u∈BV(L),we consider the weighted-fidelity learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV- Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TV-Fidω in (1.16) below, where the weight appears in the fidelity term. Let us point out that a detailed analysis of the differences arising between weighted-fidelity and weighted-regularization parameter for TV has been carried out in the one-dimensional case in [44].

In particular, the box constraint here is essential to guarantee that Level 2 of the scheme is well posed. This analysis is undertaken in Sect. 4, while the corresponding numerical study is addressed in Sect. 6.

The last theoretical result of this paper concerns replacing the TV term in our space-dependent bilevel learning schemes with a higher-order regularizer. A well-known drawback of the ROF model is the possible occurrence of staircasing effects whenever two neighboring areas of an image are both smoothed out and an abrupt spurious discontinuity is produced in the denoising process. To counteract this effect a canonical solution (among others like the use of Huber-type smoother approximations of the total variation as in [13]) consists in resorting to higher-order derivatives in the regularizer (see, e.g., [10, 21, 28, 56]). We consider here the total generalized variation (TGV) model introduced in [11], which is considered to be one of the most effective image-reconstruction models among those involving mixed first- and higher-order terms, cf. [12, 46, 56, 59] for some theoretical results about its solutions.

Theorem 1.7

(Existence of solutions to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TGV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TGVω) There exists an optimal solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^*$$\end{document}u∗ to the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TGV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TGVω in (1.20) with the minimization in (1.21) restricted by (1.29).

Analogously, we infer the ensuing theorem for the TGV with weighted fidelity.

Theorem 1.8

(Existence of solutions to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TGV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TGV-Fidω) For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in (0,1)$$\end{document}c∈(0,1), there exists an optimal solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^*$$\end{document}u∗ to the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TGV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TGV-Fidω in (1.27) with the minimization in (1.28) restricted by the box constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \left[ c,\frac{1}{c}\right] $$\end{document}λ∈c,1c.

Also in the case of weighted-TGV learning schemes, we provide a connection between stopping criteria and existence of a box constraint. To be precise, we show that if (1.29) is imposed, then a stopping criterion can be naturally imposed on the schemes. Concerning the converse implication, we show that if a suitable stopping criterion is enforced, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _L)_0$$\end{document}(αL)0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _L)_1$$\end{document}(αL)1 are both always bounded from below by a positive constant and that they cannot simultaneously blow up to infinity. The weaker nature of this latter implication is due to one main reason: the upper bound established on the optimal parameters for the weighted TV scheme is hinged upon a suitable Poincaré inequality for the total variation functional, cf. Proposition 3.5; in the TGV case, the analogous argument only provides a bound from above for the minimum between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _L)_0$$\end{document}(αL)0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _L)_1$$\end{document}(αL)1 and thus does not allow to conclude the existence of a uniform upper bound on either component, cf. Proposition 5.11. We refer to Subsect. 5.3 for a discussion of this issue and for the details of this argument. For completeness, we mention that a result related to Proposition 5.11 has been proven in [57, Proposition 6]. In Proposition 5.11, we make this study quantitative and keep track of the dependence on the cell size through the Poincaré constant.

The results we present suggest a number of possible directions and questions for future research. One possible avenue is the formulation of similar schemes with piecewise constant weights in the case of Mumford–Shah regularizations, relating to the Ambrosio–Tortorelli scheme of [40] which explicitly allows for discontinuous weights. Another is an investigation of the relation and apparent discrepancy between our results concluding stopping of the refinement of partitions, in which parameter variations at very fine scales are not advantageous, and numerical results in the literature where wildly varying parameter maps appear in the optimization, such as in [48].

The paper is organized as follows: in Sect. 2, we collect some notation which will be employed throughout the paper. The focus of Sects. 3 and 4 is on our weighted-TV scheme, as well as on the two variants thereof, including a regularization of the weight and a weighted fidelity, respectively. Section 5 is devoted to the study of our weighted-TGV learning scheme and of the corresponding TGV scheme with weighted fidelity. Section 6 contains some numerical results for the various learning schemes presented in the paper and a comparison of their performances.

Here we collect some notation that will be used throughout the paper, and introduce some energy functionals that will be studied.

The setting of our work is a two-dimensional one, mainly due to the scale invariance of the constant in the two-dimensional Poincaré–Wirtinger inequality in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV$$\end{document}BV, as discussed in the proof of Proposition 3.1. At the core of the present manuscript are weighted versions of the spaces of bounded variation and of bounded deformation. These weighted versions rely on a generalization of (2.2) and (2.3) that cannot be derived directly from the Riesz representation theorem, and thus need a careful analysis to prove the variational identities stated in (1.9) and (1.25)–(1.26), addressed in Sects. 3 and 5, respectively.

The characterization of the extension to the closed interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,+\infty ]^2$$\end{document}[0,+∞]2 of the TGV analog of (2.5), denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J(\alpha )$$\end{document}J(α) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =(\alpha _0, \alpha _1)$$\end{document}α=(α0,α1), is contained in Lemma 5.18.

In the sequel, we use both the average of a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Analysis of the Weighted-TV Learning Scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω

Here, we prove existence of solutions to the weighted-TV learning scheme, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω, introduced in (1.4). We analyze each level in the three subsequent subsections. In particular, we prove Theorem 1.5 in Subsect. 3.3. Then, in Subsect. 3.4, we prove Theorem 1.4 and we provide different examples of stopping criteria for the refinement of the admissible partitions introduced in Definition 1.2.

On Level 3

In this section, we discuss the main features of Level 3, and variants thereof, of the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4).

On Level 2

Here, we discuss existence and uniqueness of solutions to the minimization problem in (1.7). A key step in this discussion is the study of the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV_{\omega }(\Omega )$$\end{document}BVω(Ω) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω-weighted BV-functions in an open set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rn, where the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega :\Omega \rightarrow [0,\infty )$$\end{document}ω:Ω→[0,∞) is assumed to be a locally integrable function. Theorem 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rn be an open set and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega :\Omega \rightarrow [0,\infty )$$\end{document}ω:Ω→[0,∞) be a locally integrable function. Then, the following hold: (i)The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\mapsto TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )$$\end{document}u↦TVω(u,Ω) is lower-semicontinuous with respect to the (strong) convergence in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1_{\omega ,\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}Lω,loc1(Ω).(ii)Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^1_{\omega ,\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈Lω,loc1(Ω), we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )=TV_{\omega ^{sc^-}}(u,\Omega )$$\end{document}TVω(u,Ω)=TVωsc-(u,Ω), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{sc^-}$$\end{document}ωsc- denotes the lower-semicontinuous envelope of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω.(iii)Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is lower-semicontinuous and strictly positive everywhere in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} (i)The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\mapsto TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )$$\end{document}u↦TVω(u,Ω) is lower-semicontinuous with respect to the (strong) convergence in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1_{\omega ,\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}Lω,loc1(Ω).

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\mapsto TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )$$\end{document}u↦TVω(u,Ω) is lower-semicontinuous with respect to the (strong) convergence in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1_{\omega ,\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}Lω,loc1(Ω).

(ii)Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^1_{\omega ,\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈Lω,loc1(Ω), we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )=TV_{\omega ^{sc^-}}(u,\Omega )$$\end{document}TVω(u,Ω)=TVωsc-(u,Ω), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{sc^-}$$\end{document}ωsc- denotes the lower-semicontinuous envelope of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω.

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^1_{\omega ,\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈Lω,loc1(Ω), we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )=TV_{\omega ^{sc^-}}(u,\Omega )$$\end{document}TVω(u,Ω)=TVωsc-(u,Ω), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{sc^-}$$\end{document}ωsc- denotes the lower-semicontinuous envelope of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω.

(iii)Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is lower-semicontinuous and strictly positive everywhere in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω. Then, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^1_{\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈Lloc1(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )<\infty $$\end{document}TVω(u,Ω)<∞ if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_\text {loc}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L^1(\Omega ;\vert Du\vert )$$\end{document}ω∈L1(Ω;|Du|). If any of these two equivalent conditions hold, then we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} TV_{\omega }(u,B)= \int _B \omega (x)\;\text {d}|Du|(x) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TVω(u,B)=∫Bω(x)d|Du|(x) for every Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\subset \Omega $$\end{document}B⊂Ω.

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is lower-semicontinuous and strictly positive everywhere in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω. Then, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in L^1_{\text {loc}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈Lloc1(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV_{\omega }(u,\Omega )<\infty $$\end{document}TVω(u,Ω)<∞ if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in BV_\text {loc}(\Omega )$$\end{document}u∈BVloc(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L^1(\Omega ;\vert Du\vert )$$\end{document}ω∈L1(Ω;|Du|). If any of these two equivalent conditions hold, then we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} TV_{\omega }(u,B)= \int _B \omega (x)\;\text {d}|Du|(x) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TVω(u,B)=∫Bω(x)d|Du|(x) for every Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\subset \Omega $$\end{document}B⊂Ω.

Proof

The proof of (i)–(iii) may be found in [5] under the additional assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω satisfies a Muckenhoupt \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1$$\end{document}A1 condition in (3.5) (see [5] for the details). Without assuming this extra assumption on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω, the proof of (i) may be found in [15, Proposition 1.3.1 and Remark 1.3.2]; the proof of (ii) follows from [15, Proposition 2.1.1 and Theorem 2.1.2]; finally, (iii) is shown in [15, Theorem 2.1.5]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The existence and uniqueness of solutions of Level 2 of the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4) with (1.5) replaced by (1.11) are hinged on the following theorem.

Corollary 3.3

There exists a unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\mathscr {L}\in BV_{\omega _\mathscr {L}} (\Omega )\cap BV(\Omega ) \cap BV_{\omega ^{sc^-}_\mathscr {L}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}uL∈BVωL(Ω)∩BV(Ω)∩BVωLsc-(Ω) to Level 2 of the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω in (1.4) with (1.5) replaced by (1.11), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _\mathscr {L}^{sc^-}$$\end{document}ωLsc- denotes the lower-semicontinuous envelope of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _\mathscr {L}$$\end{document}ωL. Moreover,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\min \bigg \{\int _Q|u_\eta -u|^2\;\text {d}x +TV_{\omega _\mathscr {L}}(u,Q)\!:\,u\in BV_{\omega _{\mathscr {L}}}(Q)\bigg \}\\&\quad =\int _Q|u_\eta -u_\mathscr {L}|^2\;\text {d}x + \int _\Omega \omega ^{sc^-}_\mathscr {L}(x)\;\text {d}|Du_\mathscr {L}|(x). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}min{∫Q|uη-u|2dx+TVωL(u,Q):u∈BVωL(Q)}=∫Q|uη-uL|2dx+∫ΩωLsc-(x)d|DuL|(x).

Proof

Using the analysis in Subsect. 3.1, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _\mathscr {L}$$\end{document}ωL in (1.8) satisfies the bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0 \leqslant \omega _\mathscr {L}\leqslant \tfrac{1}{c_0} $$\end{document}c0⩽ωL⩽1c0 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q, which, together with Theorem 3.2, concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3.4

Recalling once again the analysis in Subsect. 3.1, the previous corollary still holds if we assume that (3.1) holds for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\in \mathscr {L}$$\end{document}L∈L instead of replacing (1.5) by (1.11).

On Level 1

Here, we prove that Level 1 of the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω admits a solution provided we consider a stopping criterion as in Definition 1.2. We start by checking that the box constraint (1.10) yields such a stopping criterion, after which we establish the converse statement. We then explore alternative stopping criteria.

To prove that the box constraint (1.10) yields a stopping criterion for the refinement of the admissible partitions, we first recall the existence of a smallness condition on the tuning parameter under which the restored image given by the TV model is constant.

Proposition 3.5

There exists a positive constant, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Q$$\end{document}CQ, depending only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q, such that for any dyadic cube \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\subset Q$$\end{document}L⊂Q and for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \geqslant C_Q \Vert u_\eta \Vert _{L^2(L)}$$\end{document}α⩾CQ‖uη‖L2(L), the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_{\alpha ,L}$$\end{document}uα,L of (1.6) is constant, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_{\alpha ,L} \equiv [ u_\eta ]_L$$\end{document}uα,L≡[uη]L.

Proof

The proof is a simple consequence of [47, Proposition 2.5.7] combined with the scaling invariance of the constant in the 2-dimensional Poincaré–Wirtinger inequality in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV$$\end{document}BV (see [2, Remark 3.50]). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proof

Remark 3.7

We have shown in the previous proof that the box-constraint condition yields a threshold on the minimum side length of the dyadic squares of the possible optimal partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}$$\end{document}L of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q. In other words, the box-constraint condition yields the following stopping criterion for the refinement of the admissible partitions:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {S})$$\end{document}(S) There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}κ∈N such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|L|\geqslant \frac{1}{4^\kappa }$$\end{document}|L|⩾14κ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\in \mathscr {L}$$\end{document}L∈L.

In the next subsection, we establish the converse of this implication (see the proof of Theorem 1.4).

We conclude this section by proving Theorem 1.5 that shows the existence of an optimal solution to the learning scheme \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω.

Proof of Theorem 1.5

This result is an immediate consequence of the results of Subsect. 3.1, Corollary 3.3, and Theorem 3.6.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Stopping Criteria and Box Constraint

In this subsection, we provide different examples of stopping criteria for the refinement of the admissible partitions, which notion was introduced in Definition 1.2, and we prove Theorem 1.4. The latter is based on the following theorem that yields a natural box constraint for the optimal parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α associated with the TV model, provided the training data satisfy some mild conditions. The proof of (3.11) in Theorem 3.8 uses arguments from [31] that are alternative to those in [34].

\usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\subset Q$$\end{document}L⊂Q a dyadic square, then there exists a positive constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_L$$\end{document}cL such that any minimizer, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^*_L$$\end{document}αL∗ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I$$\end{document}I over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,+\infty )$$\end{document}(0,+∞) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ c_L\leqslant \alpha ^*_L < C_Q\Vert \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Q$$\end{document}CQ is the constant given by Proposition 3.5. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^*_L\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}αL∗→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|L|\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}|L|→0.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TV(u_c,\Omega ) < TV(u_\eta ,\Omega )$$\end{document}TV(uc,Ω)<TV(uη,Ω);

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert u_\eta - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )} <\Vert [ u_\eta ]_\Omega - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )} $$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2<‖[uη]Ω-uc‖L2(Ω)2.

Remark 3.9

The constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\Omega $$\end{document}CΩ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Q$$\end{document}CQ characterizing the upper bound for the optimal parameters in Theorem 3.8 depend only on the domains, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q, respectively (cf. Proposition 3.5). On the other hand, the constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_\Omega $$\end{document}cΩ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_L$$\end{document}cL providing a lower bound depend not only on the corresponding domain, but also on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_c$$\end{document}uc and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\eta $$\end{document}uη.

The proof of Theorem 3.8 is hinged on the next lemma of continuity with respect to the parameter in the ROF functional, including the limit cases where the parameter vanishes or tends to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+\infty $$\end{document}+∞.

Lemma 3.10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}Ω⊂R2 be a bounded, Lipschitz domain and, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}α∈(0,+∞), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\alpha \in BV(\Omega )$$\end{document}uα∈BV(Ω) be given by (1.6) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L$$\end{document}L replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω.
