If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }=\alpha \in (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}α¯=α∈(0,+∞)2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}\subset (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}(αj)j∈N⊂(0,+∞) be any sequence such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _j \rightarrow \alpha $$\end{document}αj→α. As argued before, we observe that the uniform bounds in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV(\Omega )$$\end{document}BV(Ω) proved in Lemma 5.15 assert that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(G_{\alpha _j})_{j\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(Gαj)j∈N is an equi-coercive sequence in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1(\Omega )$$\end{document}L1(Ω). \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ-convergence on the convergence of minimizing sequences and energies (see [27, \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_\alpha $$\end{document}Gα, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\alpha _j} \rightharpoonup u_\alpha $$\end{document}uαj⇀uα weakly-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV(\Omega )$$\end{document}BV(Ω) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{j\rightarrow Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\widetilde{J}}(\alpha )&=\Vert u_{\alpha } - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )} \leqslant \liminf _{j\rightarrow \infty } \Vert u_{\alpha _j} - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )}\\ &= \liminf _{j\rightarrow \infty } J(\alpha _j). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J~(α)=‖uα-uc‖L2(Ω)2⩽lim infj→∞‖uαj-uc‖L2(Ω)2=lim infj→∞J(αj). (ii)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} }$$\end{document}αj→α¯, we have 5.38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0 \leqslant \limsup _{j\rightarrow +\infty }G_{\alpha _j}[u_{\alpha _j}]=0,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}0⩽lim )$$\end{document}L2(Ω), and in turn \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} (iii)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0=+\infty $$\end{document}α¯0=+∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_1=\alpha _1\in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}α¯1=α1∈(0,+∞), the thesis follows by observing that the same argument as in (iii) of Lemma 5.17 still holds for any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _0^j,\alpha _1^j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(α0j,α1j)j∈N with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0^j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}α0j→+∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1^j\rightarrow \alpha _1$$\end{document}α1j→α1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}j→+∞.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0=+\infty $$\end{document}α¯0=+∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_1=\alpha _1\in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}α¯1=α1∈(0,+∞), the thesis follows by observing that the same argument as in (iii) of Lemma 5.17 still holds for any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _0^j,\alpha _1^j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(α0j,α1j)j∈N with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0^j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}α0j→+∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1^j\rightarrow \alpha _1$$\end{document}α1j→α1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}j→+∞.

(iv)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0\in (0,+\infty ]$$\end{document}α¯0∈(0,+∞] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_1=0$$\end{document}α¯1=0, we can proceed exactly as in (ii) to conclude that for any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}\subset (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}(αj)j∈N⊂(0,+∞)2 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _j \rightarrow \bar{\alpha }$$\end{document}αj→α¯, we again have (5.38) by the corresponding case of Lemma 5.17.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0\in (0,+\infty ]$$\end{document}α¯0∈(0,+∞] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_1=0$$\end{document}α¯1=0, we can proceed exactly as in (ii) to conclude that for any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}\subset (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}(αj)j∈N⊂(0,+∞)2 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _j \rightarrow \bar{\alpha }$$\end{document}αj→α¯, we again have (5.38) by the corresponding case of Lemma 5.17.

(v)Analogously to (iii), if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0=\alpha _0\in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}α¯0=α0∈(0,+∞) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_1=+\infty $$\end{document}α¯1=+∞, the statement is a consequence of the fact that the same argument as in (v) of Lemma 5.17 still holds for any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _0^j,\alpha _1^j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(α0j,α1j)j∈N with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0^j\rightarrow \alpha _0$$\end{document}α0j→α0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1^j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}α1j→+∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}j→+∞.

Analogously to (iii), if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0=\alpha _0\in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}α¯0=α0∈(0,+∞) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_1=+\infty $$\end{document}α¯1=+∞, the statement is a consequence of the fact that the same argument as in (v) of Lemma 5.17 still holds for any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\alpha _0^j,\alpha _1^j)_{j\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(α0j,α1j)j∈N with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0^j\rightarrow \alpha _0$$\end{document}α0j→α0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1^j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}α1j→+∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}j→+∞.

(vi)If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_0=\bar{\alpha }_1=+\infty $$\end{document}α¯0=α¯1=+∞, by the proof item (vi) of Lemma 5.17, we have for any sequence We are now in a position to prove Theorem 5.13.

Proof of Theorem 5.13

The proof is subdivided into three steps.

Step 1. We prove that if condition i) in the statement holds, namely\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_\eta ,\Omega )- TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_c,\Omega ) >0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TGVα^0,α^1(uη,Ω)-TGVα^0,α^1(uc,Ω)>0for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\alpha }\in (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}α^∈(0,+∞)2, then there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }\in (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}α¯∈(0,+∞)2 such that5.39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert u_{\bar{\alpha }} - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )}<\Vert u_\eta - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uα¯-uc‖L2(Ω)2<‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2.From the convexity of the TGV-seminorm, arguing as in the proof of (3.14), we infer that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u_\eta -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2-\Vert u_{\alpha }-u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2&\leqslant TGV_{\alpha _0,\alpha _1}(u_\alpha ,\Omega )\\ &\quad -TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_c,\Omega ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2-‖uα-uc‖L2(Ω)2⩽TGVα0,α1(uα,Ω)-TGVα^0,α^1(uc,Ω)for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}α∈(0,+∞)2. Choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\lambda \hat{\alpha }$$\end{document}α=λα^, and denoting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\lambda (\hat{\alpha })}$$\end{document}uλ(α^) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\lambda $$\end{document}uλ, for simplicity, we find that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u_\eta -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2-\Vert u_{\lambda }-u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2&\leqslant \lambda \left( TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_\lambda ,\Omega )\right. \\ &\quad \left. -TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_c,\Omega )\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2-‖uλ-uc‖L2(Ω)2⩽λTGVα^0,α^1(uλ,Ω)-TGVα^0,α^1(uc,Ω)for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}λ∈(0,+∞). By the proof of case (ii) of Lemma 5.17 and by Corollary 5.16, it follows that, up to (non-relabelled) subsequences, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\lambda \rightarrow u_\eta $$\end{document}uλ→uη strongly in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega )$$\end{document}L2(Ω) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}λ→0. Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0; by the lower-semicontinuity of the TGV-seminorms with respect to the strong \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-convergence, we conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_\lambda ,\Omega )&\geqslant TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_\eta ,\Omega )-\varepsilon (TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1(u_\eta ,\Omega )}\\ &\quad -TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_c,\Omega )) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TGVα^0,α^1(uλ,Ω)⩾TGVα^0,α^1(uη,Ω)-ε(TGVα^0,α^1(uη,Ω)-TGVα^0,α^1(uc,Ω))for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ small enough. Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&|u_\eta -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2-\Vert u_\lambda -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2 \\&\geqslant \lambda (TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_\eta ,\Omega )\\ &\quad -TGV_{\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1}(u_c,\Omega ))(1-\varepsilon ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2-‖uλ-uc‖L2(Ω)2⩾λ(TGVα^0,α^1(uη,Ω)-TGVα^0,α^1(uc,Ω))(1-ε)for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ small enough. This implies that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\lambda }\in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}λ¯∈(0,+∞) for which\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u_\eta -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2>\Vert u_\lambda -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2>‖uλ-uc‖L2(Ω)2.The preceding estimate yields the thesis by choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }=\bar{\lambda }(\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1)$$\end{document}α¯=λ¯(α^0,α^1).

Step 2. We prove that if condition ii) in the statement holds, (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert u_\eta - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )} <\Vert \langle u_\eta \rangle - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )}$$\end{document}‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)2<‖⟨uη⟩-uc‖L2(Ω)2), then there exits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }\in (0,+\infty )^2$$\end{document}α¯∈(0,+∞)2 such that5.40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert u_{\bar{\alpha }} - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )}<\Vert \langle u_\eta \rangle - u_c\Vert ^2_{L^2(\Omega )}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uα¯-uc‖L2(Ω)2<‖⟨uη⟩-uc‖L2(Ω)2.In view of Step 1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\lambda \rightarrow 0}\Vert u_\lambda -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}=\Vert u_\eta -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}< \Vert \langle u_\eta \rangle -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limλ→0‖uλ-uc‖L2(Ω)=‖uη-uc‖L2(Ω)<‖⟨uη⟩-uc‖L2(Ω).By the proof of case (vi) of Lemma 5.17 and by Corollary 5.16, we obtain the existence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\lambda }\in (0,+\infty )$$\end{document}λ¯∈(0,+∞) for which\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u_{\bar{\lambda }}-u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}<\Vert \langle u_\eta \rangle -u_c\Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uλ¯-uc‖L2(Ω)<‖⟨uη⟩-uc‖L2(Ω).The claim follows by choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }=\bar{\lambda }(\hat{\alpha }_0,\hat{\alpha }_1)$$\end{document}α¯=λ¯(α^0,α^1).

Step 3. We conclude the proof by establishing the bounds on the parameters stated in Theorem 5.13. From the lower semicontinuity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{J} $$\end{document}J^, we infer that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^*\in [0,+\infty ]^2$$\end{document}α∗∈[0,+∞]2 where the minimum value is attained. As before, the previous existence theorem holds true under any stopping criterion for the refinement of the admissible partitions provided that the training data satisfies suitable conditions. Common Numerical Framework for all Schemes

The focus of our article is on the use of space-dependent weights and, from the numerical point of view, our schemes require addressing weights that are piecewise constant on dyadic partitions. This stands in contrast to most previous approaches for optimizing space-dependent parameters, which in most cases hinge on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1-type penalizations of the weights, as done in [25, 45] for TV, [43] for TGV and [55] for some more general convex regularizers. The piecewise constant setting makes it possible to work in a modular fashion, building upon any numerical methods that are able to compute solutions to denoising with a weight (Level 2) and finding constant optimal regularization parameters (Level 3).Fig. 2Oversmoothed denoising with a sharp change of weight and schemes corresponding to Level 2 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega _\epsilon }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVωϵ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TV-Fidω, from left to right. In our numerical examples, we have used a basic first-order finite difference discretization of the gradient and symmetrized gradient, on the regular grid arising from the discrete input images. For solving TV regularized denoising, either with constant or varying weights, we have opted for the standard primal-dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) descent scheme of [19]. The optimization for optimal constant parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α of Level 3 is done with the ‘piggyback’ version of the same algorithm, which has been proposed in [20] to learn finite difference discretizations of TV with a high degree of isotropy, and further analyzed under smoothness assumptions on the energies in [6]. Essentially, it consists in evolving an adjoint state along with the main variables, to keep track of the sensitivity of the solution with respect to parameters. We remark that such sensitivity analysis in principle requires not just first but second derivatives of the lower-level cost functions involved, in our case TV or TGV denoising involving weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^1$$\end{document}ℓ1 norms and their Fenchel conjugates, which are only componentwise piecewise smooth. In any case, as already observed in [20, Appendix A], we do achieve an adequate performance in practice. It is worth mentioning that other methods to handle the bilevel optimization problems of Level 3 in a nonsmooth setting have been introduced in [8, 32, 36]. One could also use these in our subdivision scheme within Algorithm 2, and in fact the authors of the cited papers optimize for adaptive weights on regular dyadic grids refined uniformly. In contrast, our focus here is on the adaptive subdivision scheme.

These PDHG methods are based on considering the discrete optimization problems\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min _{x \in \mathcal {X}}\, \mathcal {G}(x) + \mathcal {F}(Ky) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minx∈XG(x)+F(Ky)through their corresponding saddle point formulation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \max _{y \in \mathcal {Y}} \min _{x \in \mathcal {X}}\, \langle y, Kx \rangle _{\ell ^2} + \mathcal {G}(x) - \mathcal {F}^*(y), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}maxy∈Yminx∈X⟨y,Kx⟩ℓ2+G(x)-F∗(y),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G representing the differentiable fidelity term and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^*$$\end{document}F∗ being the projection onto a convex set, arising as the Fenchel conjugate of an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^1$$\end{document}ℓ1-type norm. In our case, we optimize the squared \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 distance to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_c$$\end{document}uc by varying the fidelity parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda = 1/\alpha $$\end{document}λ=1/α, so that6.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L(u) & = \frac{1}{2} \sum _{ij} (u^{ij} - u^{ij}_c)^2\ \text { and }\ D_\lambda \mathcal {L}(\lambda ) \nonumber \\ & = \lambda \sum _{ij} \hat{U}^{ij} (\hat{u}^{ij} - u^{ij}_\eta )\ \text { for }\ \mathcal {L}(\lambda ) = L(\hat{u}(\lambda )), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(u)=12∑ij(uij-ucij)2andDλL(λ)=λ∑ijU^ij(u^ij-uηij)forL(λ)=L(u^(λ)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{u}$$\end{document}u^, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{U}$$\end{document}U^ are the optimal image variable and corresponding adjoint obtained after convergence of (6.1) and (6.2). We have then used the derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_\lambda \mathcal {L}$$\end{document}DλL to update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ with gradient descent steps. We have chosen to not use line search, since with the piggyback algorithm evaluations of energy and of gradient for the solution of the lower level problem require a comparable amount of computational effort, that is, either performing (6.1) alone or together with (6.2) for the same number of lower level steps. We summarize this basic approach in Algorithm 1.

It is worth noting that we are optimizing only on the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ in front of the fidelity term. For the TV case and since this algorithm is applied to Level 3 with constant parameters, only the balance between the two energy terms is relevant and finding an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _L$$\end{document}λL is equivalent to finding an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _L = 1/\lambda _L$$\end{document}αL=1/λL, which can then be assembled over all L into a weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω for Level 2 of either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TV-Fidω or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TVω. In the TGV setting, optimizing only over one parameter imposes a restriction, but we have chosen to do so to keep the simple approach of Algorithm 1 and avoid more complicated behaviors of the costs when varying both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0$$\end{document}α0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1$$\end{document}α1 (or, equivalently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ together with either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0$$\end{document}α0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1$$\end{document}α1).

Effect of parameter discontinuities in Level 2 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega _\epsilon }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVωϵ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TV-Fidω

In Fig. 2, we present an example using large regularization parameters and a symmetric input image to demonstrate the effect of parameter discontinuities in Level 2 of the schemes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVω, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega _\epsilon }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVωϵ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TV-Fidω. In the weighted-TV result, a jump in the weight results in a spurious discontinuity in the resulting image. Mollifying the weight smooths the transition slightly, and it shifts it to the side with lower weight. Using a weighted fidelity term does not introduce discontinuities besides those present in the input, but still creates visible artifacts near them.

Algorithm 2Numerical approach to Level 1

Numerical approach to Level 1

Dyadic Subdivision Approach To Level 1

In Algorithm 2, we summarize our approach to numerically treat Level 1. We remark that in comparison with the original formulations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV\!}_{\omega }}$$\end{document}(LS)TVω and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TGV\!}_{\omega }}$$\end{document}(LS)TGVω as formulated in the introduction, we do not search the entire space of partitions (which would be numerically intractable) and instead work by subdivision as in Example 3.13. This means that for any given cell L, we make a local decision whether to subdivide it or not, based on the training costs arising from it before and after subdividing it in four new cells. When performing this subdivision, the parameter from the original cell is used as initialization for the optimization on the newly created ones. Even though this approach strongly restricts the number of possible partitions considered, it still manages to achieve reasonable performance in practice. On a heuristic level, this indicates that if splitting one dyadic square once to add more detail on the parameter does not lead to better performance, then in most cases it is also not advantageous to consider further finer subdivisions of the same square.

Numerical Examples with the Complete Schemes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TV-Fid}_\omega }$$\end{document}(LS)TV-Fidω and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathscr {L}}\!{\mathscr {S}})_{{TGV-Fid}_{\omega }}$$\end{document}(LS)TGV-Fidω

In Figs. 3, 4, 5, and 6, we present some illustrative examples resulting from the application of Algorithm 2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _{\max } = 4$$\end{document}ℓmax=4 to several images, for both TV and TGV regularization and optimizing for one adaptive parameter in the fidelity term, which is also shown along with the partitions overlaid on the noisy input images. In these, we generally see that the adapted fidelity parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ is higher in areas with finer details. Peak signal to noise ratios and SSIM values for each case are summarized in Table 1. In all cases, TGV with adaptive fidelity produces the best results by these metrics, but there are several instances where the gains are very marginal or there are even ties with the corresponding adaptive TV results. Nevertheless, it may be argued that even in these cases the TGV results are more visually appealing due to reduced staircasing.

For the simple example of Fig. 3, some more direct observations can be made. In it, we see that the spatially adaptive results manage to better preserve the fine structures inside the main object, while TGV greatly diminishes staircasing in regions where the original image is nearly linear. Observe that unlike the fine structures, the boundaries of the main object consisting of a sharp discontinuity along an interface with low curvature do not necessarily force further subdivision, as expected for TV or TGV regularization.

The synthetic image used in Fig. 3 was created by the authors for this article. The lighthouse and parrot examples in Figs. 4 and 6 have been cropped and converted to grayscale from images in the Kodak Lossless Image Suite. The cameraman image of Fig. 5 is very widely used, but to our knowledge its origin is not quite clear.
